ValuScope · model bake-off · October 11, 2026

Which model should write ValuScope's AI?

On ValuScope's summary pipeline, with the v0.25.0 fixes and every model running through its real API, Claude Sonnet 5.5 scored 7.7 out of 10 at 25 cents a summary and Claude Haiku 5.5 scored 6.8 at under 2 cents. Production's Gemini 3.6 Flash scored 5.2. For comp sets, Gemini 3.1 Pro picks the best peers, but the bigger win was fixing the lookup before the model, which shipped in v0.25.0.

8 models, Gemini and Claude6 real earnings calls, plus the prior quarter for each12 comp-set tickers2 blinded judges$31.14 API spend, plus subscription runs

The short version

Key Takeaways · recommended

Claude Sonnet 5.5

Scores 7.7 against 5.2 for today's Gemini 3.6 Flash, with 0.9 errors per summary and the best Q&A section after Fable. Its slowest stage was 37 seconds and it costs about 25 cents a summary, generated once per call. Opus 5.5 scores only 0.3 higher, at twice the cost and 50% slower, with more misattributions.

Key Takeaways · budget pick

Claude Haiku 5.5

Scores 6.8, ahead of every Gemini model, for about 1.5 cents a summary, a seventh of what Gemini 3.6 Flash costs today. It's also the fastest model that scores well (33-second slowest stage). The obvious choice if summaries will run at volume, such as a daily digest of thirty calls.

Comp picker · recommended

Gemini 3.1 Pro

Scores 7.9 overall, and even Claude's judge ranks it level with Opus at the top. It's paid only, about 1 cent and 9 seconds a set, and picks are cached. The free alternative is Gemini 3.8 Flash (6.2, against 5.4 for 3.6 Flash). Since v0.25.0, the model is the first source for every ticker without a curated set.

Shipped in v0.25.0

Four pipeline fixes

Q&A splitting (mis-split calls down from 20 of 52 to 5), "what changed" claims checked against the transcripts, a 300-second stage limit on Fluid compute, and comps that ask the model before Yahoo, with home groups for CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, UnitedHealth and Comcast.

Key Takeaways: scoreboard

All eight models ran through their real APIs on production's current two-pass pipeline (api/_lib/callSummary.js), after the Q&A-splitting and "what changed" fixes in v0.25.0, with only the model swapped. For each of six real calls, each model extracts this quarter's transcript and the prior quarter's, then writes the summary from those extractions alone, with quotes checked word for word against the transcript. Gemini ran at the thinking level shown and Claude at low effort, the settings you would deploy. Two judges, one from each vendor, read both transcripts and every summary blind and scored them 1 to 10.

"Production today" is what users actually see for that ticker now: a curated set, Yahoo's similar-stocks list, or similar-stocks topped up by Gemini 3.6 Flash. Findings

  • Real API timings fit comfortably. At low effort, Sonnet's slowest stage was 37 seconds, Haiku's 33 and Opus's 55. Fable needed up to 102 seconds on JPMorgan, which works only since the limit went to 300 seconds. The estimates from round two had Opus at 85 seconds; the real number is lower because low effort writes leaner extractions than the subscription subagents did.
  • Fable is the most thorough and the least phone-friendly. It scored highest on coverage (9.5) and insight (9.0), but averaged 1,640 words against the 900-word limit (concision 4.8) and costs $1.55 a summary, six times Sonnet. The extra detail isn't worth it on a phone.
  • Misattribution is the main remaining error, even for Claude. Most of Opus's errors were statements credited to the wrong executive: Apple's CFO Kevan Parekh's lines given to Tim Cook and the reverse. The extraction records who answered, but the composer sometimes reattributes. The "what changed" fix removed the previous most common error.
  • The 60-second limit was already biting. In round two, Gemini 3.6 Flash at its default thinking took 75 seconds on Apple's prior-quarter extraction, which would have failed in production. The limit was ValuScope's own maxDuration: 60, the Hobby ceiling without Fluid compute. Fluid compute is now on and the summary function has 300 seconds.
  • Gemini 3.6 Flash, today's model, is weakest where the new pipeline matters most. Its Q&A score was 4.2 in round three, against 8.2 to 8.5 for Sonnet, Opus and Fable, and 5.1 in round two. The judges said it marks nearly every answer "answered directly" and often leaves Unanswered empty, which defeats the purpose of the section. It also said Apple's CEO transition had already happened and labelled Tim Cook "Executive Chairman" throughout; the transcript has him speaking as CEO.
  • Fixed in v0.25.0: the Q&A splitter misfired on half the bake-off's transcripts. segmentTranscript looked for phrases like "Q&A session" to find where questions begin. Deere's and Chipotle's opening legal notice ("Participants in the call, including the Q&A session, agree…") and Lilly's welcome tripped it early. For Lilly the entire call became one 90-paragraph "exchange", and for Deere and Chipotle the prepared remarks were filed as exchange 1. That's 6 of the 12 transcripts. Every model except Haiku 4.5 repaired it themselves, splitting Lilly's call back into its 13 real questions, so the damage is mostly hidden today. But the pipeline is relying on the model to clean up after it, and the extraction prompt promises numbered exchanges it doesn't get. The fix keys the split on the operator opening the line and recognises more ways of introducing an analyst; on 52 real calls, mis-split ones fell from 20 to 5.
  • Fixed in v0.25.0: "what changed" invented new and dropped topics for every model. The composer compares two short label lists from the extractions without seeing either transcript. So it lists things as "new" that were discussed last quarter (JPMorgan's cash tool, Kinsale's commercial auto) and as "dropped" things discussed at length this quarter. It was the most common error even for the best models. Each claim is now checked against the transcript it is about and removed if contradicted; on round two's summaries that removed about a third of these claims, including every false one the judges flagged.
  • In round two the judges agreed on the order but not on length. Gemini's judge gave Sonnet its top score (9.7), while Claude's judge gave it 7.7, citing summaries that ran about 1,600 words against the 900-word phone limit. Opus trimmed its own drafts toward the limit and scored 8.8 on concision, the highest of any model. Both judges put all three of Opus, Fable and Sonnet clearly above every Gemini model.
  • Haiku 5.5 is not Haiku 4.5. The subscription only offers Haiku 4.5, which came last in round two (3.0). Haiku 5.5 through the API scored 6.8 in round three and 7.4 in round one, ahead of every Gemini model both times.
  • No model made up numbers in the earlier round. The figure check found every number in all 48 single-pass summaries in its transcript except one sum (24 + 176 broker appointments written as 200). The errors that remain are subtler: wrong attribution, reversed cause and effect, hedges turned into commitments, and the wrong period.
  • Sonnet 5.5 broke on comp picks. At low effort it returned two peers for Celsius plus Celsius itself, and for Hims it returned three peers and a literal "HIMS_PLACEHOLDER". It's strong at summaries and unreliable as a comp picker, so the two features shouldn't share a model just because one is good.
  • Every model suggests dead tickers. Between 6% and 11% of comp suggestions were dead or recycled symbols: SQ (Block is now XYZ), CYBR (bought by Palo Alto), UDMY, FI and OLO. Only Claude Opus 5.5 knew Block's new ticker. The live-quote check in peers.js is essential; keep it whatever model runs.
  • Fixed in v0.25.0: two comps bugs. CrowdStrike's curated set is MSFT, AMZN, GOOGL, SNOW, MDB, DDOG, NET and NVDA. getCuratedComps merges CRWD's three groups in table order and stops at eight before it reaches Cybersecurity. Toast's similar-stocks lookup returns IOT, GTLB, MDB, DOCN, RBRK and MNDY, which share Yahoo's "Software - Infrastructure" label but have nothing to do with restaurant payments. Cava's set is padded with On Running.
  • Gemini 3.1 Pro rates its own comp sets highly. Its judge gave its own sets 9.0, while Claude's judge gave them 6.8. Even on Claude's judge alone it ties Opus for first, so the pick stands, but the real margin is smaller than the overall column suggests.
  • Method notes. Three rounds. Round one used the retired single-pass prompt. Round two used the current pipeline before the fixes, with Claude run as subscription subagents because the API key had no credit. Round three, the headline, used the fixed pipeline with every model through its API at the settings you would deploy. In round three only the Claude judge has scored so far: Google's monthly spending cap on the Gemini key stopped the Gemini judge and Gemini 3.1 Pro's Deere run. In rounds one and two the Gemini judge ranked the Claude models at least as high as the Claude judge did, so a Claude judge favouring Claude is unlikely to explain the gap, but round three should get its second judge once the cap is raised. Each model ran once per input, so per-call scores are noisy; the averages are what count.

Speed

The slowest single pass for each model across the six calls. Production runs every pass as its own Vercel request, so one slow pass is a failed summary, not just a slow one. Until v0.25.0 the limit was 60 seconds; it is now 300.

The red line marks the old 60-second stage limit; bars in red crossed it, or came within five seconds.

How each output was scored

AccuracyEvery figure, attribution and claim checked against the transcript. Wrong numbers, wrong people, wrong periods and invented content pull it down hard.
Q&AThe exchanges chosen carry signal, are attributed to the right people, and the "read" on how directly each was answered is fair.
CoverageResults versus prior periods, guidance changes, strategic news, what analysts pressed on.
InsightSurfaces the surprise, the number that moved or the question management avoided, and a "what changed" that is real.
ConcisionTight and scannable on a phone, within about 900 words, no filler or repetition.
DisciplineStatements attributed by name and title, numbers with every claim, no advice, no outside knowledge.
Comp relevance, coverage, scale, noiseSame business model and economics; obvious competitors present; sensible size; no misfits or wrong tickers.

Judges: Claude Opus 5.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro, list order shuffled differently for each judge. Gemini 3.1 Pro judged through the API at high reasoning effort throughout. Claude Opus 5.5 judged through the API until the credit ran out, then as a subagent given the identical blinded prompt and nothing else. On the current pipeline the judges also saw the prior quarter's transcript, to check "what changed". Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5.5 are also contestants, so each judge marked its own output among the others without knowing which it was. In the earlier round, a separate figure check pulled every number out of each summary and looked for it in the transcript, including spoken forms like "two and a half"; across all 48 summaries only 1 figure had no source within rounding.

Read the summaries yourself

Current pipeline. Ranked by the judges' average for that call. Open a card to see the summary next to each judge's comment and the errors it named.

Mega-cap, heavily scripted call
AAPL · fiscal Q3 2026 call, reported 2026-07-30 · 108 transcript paragraphs

Claude Fable 5.19.0

Apple's fiscal Q3 2026 revenue was $109.4B, up 16% YoY and a June quarter record, with iPhone up 22% to $54.3B, Mac up 29% to $10.4B and diluted EPS of $2.02, up 29% including an $0.11 tariff refund benefit. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September quarter revenue growth to 9%-11%, citing a ~2.5pt sequential FX headwind and supply constraints that increase significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad. Tim Cook, CEO, said this was his final earnings call, with John Ternus leading future calls.

Key takeaways

  • Tim Cook, CEO, said the primary constraint is advanced-node SoC availability and called the root cause a demand forecast issue rather than a supply issue, with September a 'scrambling' quarter.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said Apple reluctantly raised prices on iPad and Mac due to a '100-year flood' in memory pricing, and that it is too early to judge elasticity.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, said services growth slowed from 16% in Q2 to 12%, driven mainly by FX, the year-ago F1 theatrical compare, mobile gaming softness, App Store model changes and the U.S. link-out ruling.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said Siri AI public beta feedback is overwhelmingly positive, but the plan for compute costs is early and not complete, with iCloud+ upgrade tiers planned for heavy users.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, declined to provide any color beyond the September quarter.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B up 16% YoY, June quarter record
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B up 22% YoY
  • Mac revenue $10.4B up 29% YoY
  • Services revenue $30.7B up 12% YoY, vs 16% in Q2
  • Gross margin 50.1% 48.1% ex-tariff refund vs 49.3% in March
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 up 29% YoY, includes $0.11 tariff refund
  • Operating expenses $19.1B up 23% YoY
  • Paid subscriptions over 1.5 billion new milestone

Financial highlights

  • Revenue $109.4B, up 16% YoY; products $78.7B, up 18%; services $30.7B, up 12%, a June quarter record.
  • iPhone $54.3B, up 22%; Mac $10.4B, up 29%; iPad $6.2B, down 6%; wearables $7.9B, up 6%.
  • Gross margin 50.1%, up 80bp sequentially with ~2pt tariff refund benefit; 48.1% ex-refund vs 49.3% in March, a 120bp decline Kevan Parekh, CFO, said was more than 100% explained by memory costs.
  • Operating expenses $19.1B, up 23% YoY on R&D; net income $29.8B and operating cash flow $34.4B, both June quarter records.
  • Capital return of $33B ($4B dividends, $25.8B buybacks); cash and marketable securities $147B against $84B debt.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September quarter revenue growth of 9%-11% YoY, including a ~2.5pt sequential FX headwind, versus the prior June guide of 14%-17%.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects iPhone revenue growth in the mid-teens and services growth largely similar to June's 12% after removing a ~2.5pt sequential FX headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided gross margin of 47%-48% including ~1pt tariff refund benefit (46.5% midpoint ex-refund, down 160bp from June ex-refund), versus 47.5%-48.5% guided for June.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided OpEx of $19.1B-$19.4B (prior $18.8B-$19.1B), OI&E around $350M (prior ~$250M) and a tax rate around 16.5% (prior ~17%).
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said memory costs will be even higher in September than June, with carry-in inventory offsets decreasing beyond September and market pricing continuing to rise.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the ~500bp deceleration implied by the 9%-11% guide is supply versus FX, and are memory sourcing options about supply or customer value?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: The guide reflects a ~2.5pt sequential FX headwind plus supply constraints increasing significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad; combining both gets close to June's growth rate. Tim Cook, CEO, added that memory costs were significantly higher in June than March, higher still in September with partial carry-in inventory offset that declines afterward, and that DRAM has three suppliers with Apple evaluating all options.
    Answered directly
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much do supply constraints hit the December quarter, and could Apple broaden silicon providers such as the Arizona fab?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook pushed back that the root cause is a demand forecast issue, not a supply or partner issue, with iPhone up 22% and Mac up 29%; supply has been pulled ahead and September will be a scrambling quarter. Apple sources over 100 million components from Arizona this year under its $600B U.S. commitment. Kevan Parekh, CFO, gave no color beyond September.
    Answered partially; December question declined
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Will Apple pursue multi-year supplier LTAs, is pricing meant to protect gross profit dollars or margin percent, and why did services growth of 12% miss and decelerate further?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said prices were raised reluctantly amid exponential memory increases and pricing weighs units, revenue and margin over the long term, not a formula; LTAs were not addressed. Kevan Parekh, CFO, attributed the 16% to 12% decel to FX, the F1 theatrical compare, mobile gaming softness, App Store model changes and the U.S. link-out ruling, with FX a ~5pt headwind March to September and another 2.5pt June to September.
    Answered partially
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: Status of Siri AI in China and Europe, and the FX versus commodity split in the gross margin decline from 48% ex-refund to the 46.5% midpoint?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Siri AI is not yet enabled in the EU for iPhone/iPad but is or will be on Mac; China approved original Apple Intelligence features last week, with more work needed for Siri AI. Kevan Parekh, CFO, said the 160bp June-to-September ex-refund decline is more than explained by memory, partly offset by carry-in inventory, lower non-memory costs and mix, with FX minimal.
    Answered directly
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI change capital intensity, and how is the competitive landscape evolving with OpenAI or SpaceX AI potentially building devices?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Apple runs a hybrid of third-party cloud and own data centers; AI spend sits in both OpEx and COGS, and the net cost impact of Siri AI, offset by iCloud plan upgrades, remains uncertain. John Ternus, Incoming CEO, said Apple is focused on its own plans.
    Declined to quantify

Unanswered

  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much supply constraints affect the December quarter. Kevan Parekh, CFO, said no color is being provided beyond September.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: How Siri AI usage informs compute costs and recovery via iCloud+. Tim Cook, CEO, said it is early and Apple does not have a complete plan.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: How the competitive landscape evolves with OpenAI or SpaceX AI building devices. John Ternus, Incoming CEO, answered with generalities about focusing on Apple's plans.
  • Aaron Rakers, Wells Fargo: Whether price increases have driven pull-forward or demand elasticity. Tim Cook, CEO, said it is too early to judge given channel inventory and consumer lag.

Notable quotes

  • “The root cause of it is not a regular supply issue. It's a demand forecast issue, to be candid” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “On the pricing front, we reluctantly raised prices, I would say.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “It's obviously early going for us. I don't want to say that we have a complete plan for that.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “Beyond September, we're not providing any color at this point.” Kevan Parekh, CFO

What changed

  • Guidance: Revenue growth guide: 14%-17% for June to 9%-11% for September.
  • Guidance: Gross margin guide: 47.5%-48.5% to 47%-48% (including ~1pt tariff refund benefit).
  • Guidance: OpEx guide: $18.8B-$19.1B to $19.1B-$19.4B.
  • Guidance: OI&E guide: ~$250M to ~$350M; tax rate ~17% to ~16.5%.
  • Guidance: Supply constraints: majority on Mac models in June to increasing significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad in September.
  • New mentions: Siri AI and iOS 27, Apple Upgrade leasing program (Klarna partnership), Broadcom American Manufacturing Program agreement exceeding $30B, Morgan Stanley, Disney and Crédit Agricole enterprise deployments
  • Dropped: Greater China performance and India opportunity, Google collaboration on AI models, Capital allocation and end of net cash neutral target
  • Tone: Tim Cook's language moved from 'off the charts' demand and being 'over the moon' about India to 'scrambling' on supply, 'reluctantly' raising prices and a '100-year flood' in memory, while Kevan Parekh declined any color beyond September.

Risks mentioned

  • Advanced-node SoC supply constraints broadening to iPhone, Mac and iPad with limited supply chain flexibility (Tim Cook, CEO).
  • Memory cost inflation rising beyond September with diminishing carry-in inventory offset (Tim Cook, CEO).
  • FX headwind of ~2.5pt sequential to total growth and ~5pt March-to-September to services (Kevan Parekh, CFO).
  • Services pressure from mobile gaming softness, App Store business model changes and the U.S. link-out court ruling pending Supreme Court appeal (Kevan Parekh, CFO).
  • Regulatory hurdles for Siri AI in the EU and China and uncertain AI compute costs (Tim Cook, CEO).
Claude Opus 5.5 · 9.0

The most accurate and complete note, with correct attributions, ex-refund margin bridges, the change in supply-constraint scope and a well-evidenced tone shift, though it runs long.

Claude Sonnet 5.58.0

Apple reported June-quarter revenue of $109.4B, up 16% year over year, with diluted EPS of $2.02, up 29% and including a $0.11 tariff refund benefit. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September-quarter revenue growth to 9%-11% and gross margin to 47%-48%, citing larger supply constraints and memory costs. Tim Cook, CEO, said the root cause of the constraints is a demand forecast miss.

Key takeaways

  • Revenue was $109.4B, up 16%, with iPhone at $54.3B (up 22%) and Mac at $10.4B (up 29%).
  • iPad revenue fell 6% to $6.2B, and services growth slowed to 12% ($30.7B) from 16% last quarter.
  • Company gross margin was 50.1%, or 48.1% excluding about 2 points of tariff refund benefit.
  • Parekh guided September revenue growth to 9%-11%, with supply constraints rising significantly across iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • Cook said memory pricing is a '100-year flood' and expects costs to rise further beyond September.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B up 16% YoY
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B up 22% YoY
  • Mac revenue $10.4B up 29% YoY
  • iPad revenue $6.2B down 6% YoY
  • Services revenue $30.7B up 12% YoY
  • Company gross margin 50.1% 48.1% ex-refund; up 80bp sequentially
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 up 29% YoY, includes $0.11 refund benefit
  • Capital returned $33B $4B dividends, $25.8B buybacks

Financial highlights

  • Net income was $29.8B and operating cash flow $34.4B.
  • Operating expenses were $19.1B, up 23% year over year.
  • Products revenue was $78.7B, up 18%, and products gross margin was 40.1%, up 140bp sequentially.
  • Services gross margin was 75.6%, down 110bp sequentially.
  • Apple returned $33B ($4B dividends, $25.8B buybacks) and held $147B in cash and securities against $84B of debt.

Guidance

  • Parekh guides September revenue growth of 9%-11%, with a FX headwind of about 2.5 points sequentially.
  • Parekh expects iPhone revenue up mid-teens and services growth similar to the June reported rate after removing the FX headwind.
  • Parekh guides gross margin of 47%-48%, including about 1 point of tariff refund benefit.
  • Parekh guides operating expenses of $19.1B-$19.4B, OIE around $350M and a tax rate around 16.5%.
  • Cook expects memory costs even higher in September and market pricing to keep rising beyond; the dividend is $0.27 per share, payable August 13.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the roughly 500bp deceleration implied by September guidance is supply versus FX, and are constraints broadening beyond SoCs?
    Tim Cook / Kevan Parekh, CEO / CFO: Parekh cited about 2.5 points of sequential FX headwind plus significantly higher supply constraints across iPhone, Mac and iPad. Cook said June constraints were mainly Mac, from advanced-node SoCs, and that memory costs were significantly higher in June and will rise further in September, partly offset by carry-in inventory and lower non-memory costs. He said DRAM has three suppliers and Apple is evaluating all options.
    Answered partially; no split of supply versus FX was quantified.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much will supply constraints hit the December quarter, and could Apple broaden silicon providers?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said the constraint is advanced nodes, caused by a demand forecast miss, and September will be a scramble. Parekh gave no guidance beyond September. Cook said it is not a partner or supplier issue and that Apple sources over 100 million components from Arizona this year, under the $600B commitment.
    Answered partially; December impact and adding providers were not addressed.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Why did services growth slow to 12% and imply below 10% next quarter, and is AI pulling time from the App Store?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Parekh said growth slowed from 16% to 12% mainly on FX, no F1 theatrical release this year, and softer mobile gaming in the App Store, plus App Store model changes and the US link-out ruling. FX is about a 5 point headwind from March to September, plus 2.5 points sequentially.
    Answered partially; the AI cannibalisation and supplier agreement questions were skipped.
  • Aaron Rakers, Wells Fargo: Have you seen demand pull-forward from consumers or enterprise?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said pull-forward is not obvious given 22% iPhone growth year to date, and that iPad and Mac price elasticity after price increases is too early to judge, with more clarity in the weeks ahead.
    Answered partially; disputed the premise and gave no elasticity data.
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What is the Siri AI status in China and Europe, and what drives gross margin from 48% to about 46.5%?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Cook said there is no EU launch yet and Apple is working with the commission; China approved the original Apple Intelligence features last week and Siri AI needs more work. Parekh said the 120bp decline from March to June and 160bp from June to midpoint are each more than explained by memory cost, with minimal FX.
    Answered directly.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What effect does Apple Upgrade have, and what has the Siri AI beta shown about compute costs?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said Apple Upgrade is a leasing plan offered in retail stores, with early feedback positive; Parekh said it is US only. Cook said the compute cost plan is early and incomplete, with iCloud+ tiers expected for heavy users.
    Answered partially; no holiday demand comment.

Unanswered

  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI change Apple's capital intensity, and how does Ternus see competitors? Cook said the cost impact of Siri AI is unknown, and Ternus did not address competitors.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Is Apple pursuing multi-year LTAs, and does pricing protect gross profit dollars or margin? LTAs went unaddressed; Cook said there is no formula for pricing.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: Could Apple broaden silicon providers, and how large is the December quarter hit? Parekh gave no guidance beyond September, and adding providers was not addressed.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What does the Siri AI beta imply for holiday iPhone demand? There was no direct comment on holiday demand.

Notable quotes

  • “we're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “The root cause of it is not a regular supply issue. It's a demand forecast issue, to be candid” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “I don't want to say that we have a complete plan for that.” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Revenue growth guidance moved from 14%-17% (June quarter) to 9%-11% (September quarter).
  • Guidance: Gross margin guidance moved from 47.5%-48.5% to 47%-48%.
  • Guidance: Operating expenses guidance moved from $18.8B-$19.1B to $19.1B-$19.4B.
  • Guidance: OIE moved from around $250M to around $350M, and the tax rate from around 17% to around 16.5%.
  • New mentions: Klarna, Broadcom agreement, Ask to Browse, Crédit Agricole, iOS 27
  • Dropped: Capital return and net cash neutral target, Google collaboration
  • Tone: Cook moved from saying memory costs would have an increasing impact to calling it a '100-year flood' and a quarter of 'scrambling' on supply. He also now attributes the constraints to a demand forecast miss.

Risks mentioned

  • Memory costs rose significantly in June, are expected higher in September, and market pricing is expected to keep rising beyond.
  • Supply constraints on advanced-node SoCs increase significantly in September across iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • FX is a sequential headwind of about 2.5 points to September growth.
  • Services face App Store model changes and the US link-out court ruling, with the Supreme Court to hear the appeal.
  • Siri AI has no EU launch yet, needs more work in China, and its compute cost plan is incomplete.
Claude Opus 5.5 · 8.0

Accurate and well-judged on Q&A reads and the unanswered list, but it never mentions Cook's final call or the CEO transition, and its new-mentions list is bare labels.

  • Tone shift says Cook 'now attributes the constraints to a demand forecast miss' as if this were new, but in the prior call Cook already said 'we just undercalled the demand'.

Gemini 3.8 Flash6.0

Apple reported fiscal Q3 2026 total revenue of $109.4 billion, an increase of 16% year-over-year, alongside record net income of $29.8 billion and diluted EPS of $2.02. Results were driven by 22% growth in iPhone to $54.3 billion and 29% growth in Mac to $10.4 billion, while the company absorbed mounting memory component inflation and supply shortages. Tim Cook marked his final earnings call before incoming CEO John Ternus takes the helm.

Key takeaways

  • Total revenue reached a June-quarter record $109.4 billion, up 16% year-over-year, driven by products expanding 18% to $78.7 billion.
  • Gross margin reached 50.1%, aided by an approximate 2.0 percentage point benefit from tariff refunds.
  • Tim Cook confirmed his retirement from earnings calls, transitioning leadership to incoming CEO John Ternus.
  • Severe supply bottlenecks in advanced node SoC capacity are affecting iPhone, Mac, and iPad deliveries into the September quarter.
  • Rising DRAM costs prompted selective hardware price increases, which Tim Cook characterized as driven by a '100-year flood' in memory inflation.

Key metrics

  • Total revenue $109.4 billion Up 16% year-over-year; June quarter record
  • Diluted earnings per share $2.02 Up 29% year-over-year; includes $0.11 tariff refund benefit
  • Company gross margin 50.1% Up 80 basis points sequentially; includes ~2 percentage point tariff benefit
  • iPhone revenue $54.3 billion Up 22% year-over-year; June quarter record
  • Mac revenue $10.4 billion Up 29% year-over-year; June quarter record
  • Services revenue $30.7 billion Up 12% year-over-year; June quarter record
  • Operating cash flow $34.4 billion June quarter record
  • Active installed base Over 2.5 billion active devices All-time high

Financial highlights

  • iPhone revenue set a June-quarter record of $54.3 billion, growing 22% year-over-year.
  • Mac revenue rose 29% year-over-year to $10.4 billion, while iPad revenue declined 6% to $6.2 billion.
  • Services revenue increased 12% year-over-year to a June-quarter record $30.7 billion, supported by over 1.5 billion paid subscriptions.
  • Operating expenses totaled $19.1 billion, representing a 23% increase year-over-year.
  • Operating cash flow reached a June-quarter record of $34.4 billion, supporting $33 billion in total capital returns including $25.8 billion in share buybacks.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Q4 FY2026 total company revenue growth of 9% to 11% year-over-year.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, projects foreign exchange to create an approximate 2.5 percentage point sequential headwind to total company revenue growth in Q4 FY2026.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, forecasts Q4 FY2026 iPhone revenue to grow in the mid-teens year-over-year.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides Q4 FY2026 gross margin to 47% to 48%, which includes an approximate 1 percentage point tariff refund benefit.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Q4 FY2026 operating expenses between $19.1 billion and $19.4 billion, with an estimated tax rate of roughly 16.5%.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: Can you explain how much of the September revenue growth deceleration is supply constraints versus FX, and provide details on memory sourcing flexibility to address inflation?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Kevan Parekh, CFO, explained that foreign exchange imposes an approximate 2.5 percentage point drag alongside higher sequential supply constraints across iPhone, Mac, and iPad, while Tim Cook, CEO, noted memory costs rose in June, will rise further in September, and that having more than three DRAM suppliers offers uncertain pricing relief.
    answered directly
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What are your expected adoption rates for the Apple Upgrade Program, and how are early public beta learnings on Siri AI informing compute cost planning and monetization via iCloud+?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook, CEO, noted positive feedback on Apple Upgrade and shared that heavy Siri AI compute users may buy up the stack on iCloud+ plans, while Kevan Parekh, CFO, noted the upgrade program is currently U.S.-only, though specific adoption rates were omitted.
    answered partially
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How significantly will supply constraints impact the December quarter, and could Apple broaden silicon suppliers, such as the fab in Arizona, to alleviate constraints?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook, CEO, explained that advanced node SoC shortages stem from unexpectedly high demand rather than supplier failure and cited over 100 million Arizona-sourced components this year, while Kevan Parekh, CFO, declined to provide December quarter guidance.
    answered partially
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: What is the thought process behind recent price increases and multi-year LTAs, and what factors are driving the deceleration in services growth into the September quarter?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook, CEO, stated hardware price increases were reluctantly enacted due to exponential memory cost inflation, and Kevan Parekh, CFO, attributed Services deceleration to a 2.5 percentage point sequential FX drag, a tough F1 film comparison, mobile gaming softness, and adjustments to U.S. link-out rulings.
    answered directly
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What is the status of launching Siri AI in Europe and China, and what is driving the sequential gross margin moderation in guidance between FX and memory costs?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook, CEO, reported ongoing European Commission discussions with Mac already eligible, and noted China approved baseline Apple Intelligence last week while Siri AI requires additional work; Kevan Parekh, CFO, stated memory costs drive virtually the entire sequential margin decline from 48.1% to the 46.5% midpoint excluding tariff refunds.
    answered directly
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI alter Apple's capital intensity given its distributed architecture, and how does the incoming CEO view potential competitive threats from emerging AI devices?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook, CEO, detailed Apple's hybrid infrastructure model across first-party and third-party data centers where costs might be offset by iCloud plans, while John Ternus, incoming CEO, offered general enthusiasm about opportunities without addressing specific device threats.
    answered partially

Unanswered

  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What are your expected adoption rates for the Apple Upgrade Program, and how are early public beta learnings on Siri AI informing compute cost planning and monetization via iCloud+? Management provided qualitative color on retail leasing and iCloud+ tiers but omitted specific adoption rate projections.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How significantly will supply constraints impact the December quarter, and could Apple broaden silicon suppliers, such as the fab in Arizona, to alleviate constraints? Kevan Parekh, CFO, explicitly declined to give guidance or impact estimates beyond the September quarter.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI alter Apple's capital intensity given its distributed architecture, and how does the incoming CEO view potential competitive threats from emerging AI devices? John Ternus, incoming CEO, answered in broad terms about internal focus and industry opportunity rather than evaluating emerging device competition.

Notable quotes

  • “As you know, this will be my final earnings call, and John will lead these calls going forward.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We did it because we're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases in memory prices.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “Of course, the ability to run some percentage of request on device is also very strategic and sort of a competitive weapon, if you will.” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Total company revenue year-over-year growth: guided to 9%-11% for Q4 FY2026, compared to 14% to 17% guided for Q3 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Gross margin: guided to 47%-48% for Q4 FY2026, compared to 47.5% to 48.5% guided for Q3 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Operating expenses: guided to $19.1 billion-$19.4 billion for Q4 FY2026, compared to $18.8 billion to $19.1 billion guided for Q3 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Other Income and Expense (OI&E): guided to ~$350 million for Q4 FY2026, up from around $250 million guided for Q3 FY2026.
  • Guidance: Tax rate: guided to ~16.5% for Q4 FY2026, compared to around 17% guided for Q3 FY2026.
  • New mentions: Broadcom agreement, Ask to Browse, Time Allowances, Cape Fear, Klarna
  • Dropped: Abandonment of net cash neutral target, Greater China and India specific regional performance metrics, Perplexity partnership, Google foundational models collaboration, Apple Maps advertising launch
  • Tone: The tone shifted from confidence in handling early component pressure to acute caution regarding severe supply constraints and exponential memory inflation, alongside a formal transition of executive authority.

Risks mentioned

  • Severe advanced node SoC foundry capacity constraints causing product shortages across iPhone, Mac, and iPad.
  • Exponentially rising DRAM memory prices negatively impacting hardware gross margins into the September quarter.
  • A sequential foreign exchange growth headwind of approximately 2.5 percentage points.
  • Regulatory adjustments regarding U.S. link-outs and delayed rollout of Siri AI features under European Commission scrutiny.

Claude Haiku 5.56.0

Apple reported fiscal Q3 2026 revenue of $109.4B, up 16% YoY, with iPhone at $54.3B, up 22%. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September-quarter total revenue growth to 9%-11%, citing supply constraints and about a 2.5 point sequential FX headwind. Tim Cook, CEO, said this will be his final earnings call and John Ternus will lead future calls.

Key takeaways

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, said September revenue growth of 9%-11% reflects about 2.5 points of sequential FX headwind plus supply constraints expected to increase significantly.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said the primary constraint is availability of advanced nodes for SoCs, and that demand exceeded forecast for iPhone and Mac.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said memory costs will be even higher in the September quarter and expects memory pricing to keep rising beyond September.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, said the September-quarter gross margin guide of 47%-48% includes about 1 point of benefit from tariff refunds.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said Apple sources over 100 million components this year from Arizona under the $600 billion US commitment.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B up 16% YoY; June quarter record
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B up 22% YoY; June quarter record
  • Mac revenue $10.4B up 29% YoY; June quarter record
  • iPad revenue $6.2B down 6% YoY
  • Company gross margin 50.1% up 80 bps sequentially; includes about 2 point tariff refund benefit
  • Operating expenses $19.1B up 23% YoY
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 up 29% YoY; includes $0.11 tariff refund benefit
  • Capital returned to shareholders $33B $4B dividends and equivalents; $25.8B share repurchases

Financial highlights

  • Revenue $109.4B, up 16% YoY, a June-quarter record.
  • Products revenue $78.7B, up 18%; Services revenue $30.7B, up 12%, a June-quarter record.
  • Company gross margin 50.1%, up 80 bps sequentially, including about a 2 point tariff refund benefit.
  • Products gross margin 40.1%, up 140 bps sequentially; Services gross margin 75.6%, down 110 bps sequentially.
  • Diluted EPS $2.02, up 29% YoY, including a $0.11 tariff refund benefit; net income $29.8B.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides September-quarter total company revenue growth to 9%-11% YoY, with about a 2.5 point sequential FX headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides September-quarter iPhone revenue growth to mid-teens (reported YoY) and gross margin to 47%-48%, including about 1 point of tariff refund benefit.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides September-quarter services growth to be largely similar to the June quarter after removing about a 2.5 point sequential FX headwind; June-quarter services growth was 12%.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides September-quarter operating expenses to $19.1B-$19.4B, other income/expense to around $350M excluding minority investment mark-to-market, and tax rate to around 16.5%.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, declared a quarterly dividend of $0.27 per share, payable August 13, 2026, record date August 10, 2026.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the September 9%-11% growth deceleration is supply constraints versus FX, and what are the supply constraints broadening beyond advanced SoCs?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: Parekh said September growth reflects about 2.5 points of sequential FX headwind plus supply constraints expected to increase significantly, affecting iPhone, Mac and iPad; combined, close to June-quarter growth. Cook said June constraints were primarily Mac, less on iPhone and iPad. Parekh said memory costs will be higher in September, partly offset by carry-in inventory, and that the DRAM market has three suppliers; Apple is evaluating all options.
    partial
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What adoption rates are expected for the Apple Upgrade Program, and will Siri AI drive holiday iPhone demand?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said Apple Upgrade makes latest products easier to get via a leasing plan with residual values much higher than competitors; uptake is yet to be seen. Parekh said it is currently available only in the U.S. Cook said there is no complete plan yet for Siri AI compute costs, expects some users to use it heavily, and plans iCloud+ upgrade tiers.
    partial
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: Street models December-quarter growth decelerating to 8%-9%; how much will supply constraints hit December?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said the primary constraint is advanced nodes for SoCs, that demand exceeded forecast for iPhone and Mac, and that supply has been pulled ahead. Parekh said the September guidance includes these dynamics and FX, with no color beyond September.
    partial
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: What drives services deceleration from 16% to 12% and the implied sub-10% fiscal Q4 growth, including App Store effects?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Parekh said services deceleration is mainly sequential FX, with the prior-year F1 theatrical release lapped. App Store faced mobile gaming headwinds and business model changes in some countries, and the U.S. link-out ruling is being appealed to the Supreme Court; App Store still set a June-quarter record. Parekh expects about a 5 point FX headwind to year-over-year services growth from March to September quarter.
    partial
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What hurdles remain for launching Siri AI in China and Europe, and what drove the sequential gross margin moderation to the 46.5% guidance midpoint?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Cook said Apple is working closely with the EU Commission, launching in the EU is not yet possible, and Mac will get Siri AI there. In China, approval was received last week for original Apple Intelligence features, with more work needed for Siri AI. Parekh said the memory cost change explains more than 100% of the sequential gross margin change from 49.3% in March to 48.1% in June (adjusted), with FX a minimal factor.
    direct

Unanswered

  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much will supply constraints hit the December quarter? Parekh gave no color beyond September, deferring to the September guidance.
  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How is memory sourcing flexibility aimed: securing supply or mitigating memory inflation and preserving customer value? Cook said Apple is evaluating all options; no specific sourcing approach was described.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Will Siri AI change Apple's capital intensity given the on-device and back-end compute mix? Cook said the cost impact of Siri AI is uncertain and described a hybrid model with third-party cloud and its own data centers, without quantifying capital intensity.

Notable quotes

  • “We're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases in memory prices.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We reluctantly raised prices, I would say.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “It's not obvious in the data that what you're asking is true.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “this will be my final earnings call, and John will lead these calls going forward.” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Services growth guide: prior June-quarter guide was similar to March-quarter rate after removing FX tailwind (March services 16%); current September-quarter guide is largely similar to June-quarter rate (12%) after removing about 2.5 point FX headwind. These are different periods, not a revision of the same figure.
  • Guidance: Operating expenses: prior June-quarter guide $18.8B-$19.1B; current September-quarter guide $19.1B-$19.4B. Different periods.
  • Guidance: Tax rate: prior June-quarter guide around 17%; current September-quarter guide around 16.5%. Different periods.
  • Guidance: Gross margin: prior June-quarter guide 47.5%-48.5%; current September-quarter guide 47%-48% including about 1 point tariff refund benefit. Different periods.
  • New mentions: iOS 27, Klarna, Pinellas County Schools, Crédit Agricole
  • Dropped: Capital allocation and net cash neutral change, Greater China and India demand
  • Tone: Prior-quarter remarks centred on demand enthusiasm and constraints in the March quarter; this call used more hedged language on supply, memory pricing and Siri AI timing, with Cook describing constraints as very significant and the supply chain as less flexible than normal.

Risks mentioned

  • Supply constraints on advanced nodes for SoCs expected to increase significantly, affecting iPhone, Mac and iPad.
  • Memory costs expected to rise beyond September, with DRAM supply concentrated among three suppliers.
  • About 2.5 point sequential FX headwind to total company growth and about 5 point FX headwind to services year-over-year growth from March to September quarter.
  • App Store mobile gaming headwinds, business model changes in some countries, and the U.S. link-out ruling, which Supreme Court will hear on appeal.
  • Siri AI compute costs not yet planned; cost impact described as uncertain.

Claude Opus 5.56.0

Apple reported June-quarter revenue of $109.4B, up 16% YoY and a June-quarter record, with iPhone up 22% and Mac up 29%, while gross margin of 50.1% included about 2 points of tariff refund benefit. Kevan Parekh, CFO, guided September-quarter growth down to 9%-11% on roughly 2.5 points of FX headwind and significantly increased supply constraints, with gross margin falling to a 46.5% midpoint ex-refund on memory costs. Tim Cook, CEO, said this is his final earnings call, with John Ternus leading future calls.

Key takeaways

  • Tim Cook, CEO, attributed the advanced-node SoC supply constraints to a demand forecast miss, not a supplier issue, as iPhone (+22%) and Mac (+29%) beat expectations.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said Apple 'reluctantly' raised prices on Mac and iPad because of a '100-year flood' in memory pricing.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, said memory costs will be even higher in September and keep rising beyond it, while the carry-in inventory offset diminishes.
  • Services growth slowed from 16% to 12%, which Tim Cook, CEO, attributed mainly to FX plus F1 lapping, mobile gaming softness, App Store model changes and the U.S. link-out ruling.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said Siri AI public beta feedback is overwhelmingly positive but compute cost plans are incomplete; iCloud+ upgrade tiers will target heavy users.

Key metrics

  • Revenue $109.4B +16% YoY, June quarter record
  • iPhone revenue $54.3B +22% YoY
  • Mac revenue $10.4B +29% YoY
  • Services revenue $30.7B +12% YoY
  • Gross margin 50.1% +80bp seq; 48.1% ex-refund
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 +29% YoY; incl $0.11 tariff refund
  • Operating expenses $19.1B +23% YoY
  • Capital returned $33B $4B dividends, $25.8B buybacks

Financial highlights

  • Revenue was $109.4B (+16% YoY), with products at $78.7B (+18%) and services at $30.7B (+12%).
  • iPhone revenue was $54.3B (+22%), Mac $10.4B (+29%), iPad $6.2B (-6%) and wearables, home & accessories $7.9B (+6%).
  • Gross margin was 50.1% (48.1% ex-refund); products 40.1% and services 75.6% (-110bp sequential).
  • Diluted EPS was $2.02 (+29% YoY, including $0.11 tariff refund) on net income of $29.8B.
  • Operating cash flow was $34.4B and $33B was returned, including $25.8B in buybacks and $4B in dividends.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides September-quarter revenue growth of 9%-11% YoY, including about 2.5 points of sequential FX headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects iPhone growth in the mid-teens and services growth similar to June's 12% after removing the FX headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides gross margin of 47%-48% including about 1 point of tariff refund benefit (46.5% midpoint ex-refund).
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides opex of $19.1B-$19.4B, OIE of about $350M and a tax rate of about 16.5%.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, said the multi-year Broadcom U.S. silicon agreement is expected to exceed $30B.

Q&A

  • Amit Daryanani, Evercore: How much of the roughly 500bp deceleration to the 9-11% guide is supply versus FX, and is memory sourcing flexibility about supply or pricing?
    Kevan Parekh, CFO: About 2.5 points of FX plus significantly increased iPhone, Mac and iPad supply constraints bridge to June's growth rate; memory costs rose significantly in June, will be higher in September and keep rising, partly offset by a diminishing carry-in benefit; DRAM has three suppliers and more could help supply, though the pricing effect is unclear.
    Answered directly.
  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: How much will supply constraints hit the December quarter, and could Apple broaden silicon suppliers such as the Arizona fab?
    Tim Cook, CEO: He gave no guidance beyond September and pushed back on the supplier premise, saying the root cause is a demand forecast miss; Apple sources over 100 million components from Arizona this year as part of its $600B U.S. commitment.
    Declined to quantify; disputed the premise.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Will Apple pursue multi-year LTAs, does pricing protect gross profit dollars or margin, and what drove the services deceleration?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Prices were raised reluctantly and Apple weighs units, revenue and margin with no formula; the LTA question went unaddressed; the services drop from 16% to 12% was mainly FX (about 5 points from March to September), plus F1 lapping, gaming softness and App Store changes, though the App Store still set a June record.
    Answered partially.
  • Samik Chatterjee, JPMorgan: What is Siri AI's launch status in China and the EU, and what drives the gross margin decline to a 46.5% midpoint?
    Tim Cook, CEO: In the EU, Siri AI will be offered on Mac while Apple works with the Commission; China approved the original Apple Intelligence features last week, with more work needed for Siri AI; Kevan Parekh, CFO, said memory explains more than 100% of the 120bp and 160bp margin steps down, with minimal FX impact.
    Answered directly.
  • Michael Ng, Goldman Sachs: What adoption is expected for Apple Upgrade, and what do Siri AI compute costs mean for iCloud+?
    Tim Cook, CEO: Apple Upgrade leasing is U.S. only and in retail stores, with early feedback positive and uptake still to be determined; there is no complete plan yet for compute costs, but iCloud+ upgrade tiers will be offered for heavy users.
    Answered partially.
  • Aaron Rakers, Wells Fargo: Was demand pulled forward ahead of memory-driven price increases?
    Tim Cook, CEO: He said pull-forward is not obvious in the data, and the Mac and iPad price increases are too recent to judge elasticity given channel inventory, with more to learn in coming weeks.
    Answered partially.

Unanswered

  • Ben Reitzes, Melius Research: The impact of supply constraints on the December quarter. Tim Cook, CEO, said Apple gives no guidance beyond September.
  • Erik Woodring, Morgan Stanley: Whether Apple will sign multi-year memory LTAs. The question was not addressed; Tim Cook, CEO, spoke only about pricing trade-offs.
  • Wamsi Mohan, Bank of America: Whether Siri AI changes capital intensity, and John Ternus's view of the OpenAI and SpaceX device competition. Tim Cook, CEO, called the cost impact uncertain, and John Ternus, Incoming CEO, said only that Apple is focused on its own plans.

Notable quotes

  • “It's a demand forecast issue, to be candid, where the iPhone and the Mac are both doing remarkably better than we thought they would do.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We did it because we're in what I would characterize as a 100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases in memory prices.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “In terms of what it means for compute cost, it's obviously early going for us. I don't want to say that we have a complete plan for that.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “We've got a quarter that we're going to be scrambling on the supply side, essentially.” Tim Cook, CEO
  • “As you know, this will be my final earnings call, and John will lead these calls going forward.” Tim Cook, CEO

What changed

  • Guidance: Revenue growth: 14%-17% (June quarter) to 9%-11% (September quarter).
  • Guidance: Gross margin: 47.5%-48.5% to 47%-48% including about 1 point of refund benefit (46.5% midpoint ex-refund).
  • Guidance: Operating expenses: $18.8B-$19.1B to $19.1B-$19.4B.
  • Guidance: OI&E: about $250M to about $350M.
  • Guidance: Tax rate: about 17% to about 16.5%.
  • Guidance: Services growth: similar to March's 16% ex-FX, to similar to June's 12% ex-FX.
  • New mentions: iOS 27, Broadcom $30B agreement, iCloud+ upgrade tiers, Ask to Browse / Time Allowances, Arizona fab, Morgan Stanley/Disney/Crédit Agricole enterprise
  • Dropped: Capital allocation and the end of the net cash neutral target, Google AI collaboration, Advertising and Maps ads
  • Tone: Last quarter Tim Cook, CEO, said Apple would 'look at a range of options' on memory costs and declined to go further; this quarter he confirmed price increases taken 'reluctantly' and described a quarter of 'scrambling' on supply.

Risks mentioned

  • Advanced-node SoC supply constraints on iPhone, Mac and iPad, with limited supply chain flexibility.
  • Memory costs rising beyond September as the carry-in inventory offset diminishes.
  • An FX headwind of about 2.5 points sequentially to total growth and about 5 points to services from March to September.
  • Mobile gaming softness, App Store model changes and the U.S. link-out ruling, which the Supreme Court will hear on appeal.
  • Rising AI spending in opex and COGS, with an uncertain Siri AI cost impact and regulatory work still needed in the EU and China.

Apple Inc. reported June quarter total revenue of $109.4 billion, representing a 16% year-over-year increase and setting a June quarter record. Tim Cook, CEO, announced that this was his final earnings call before John Ternus assumes leadership of future calls. Kevan Parekh, CFO, provided September quarter guidance for total revenue growth of 9% to 11% year-over-year, citing supply constraints and a 2.5 percentage point foreign exchange headwind.

Key takeaways

  • Tim Cook, CEO, noted that fiscal Q3 2026 marked his final earnings call as CEO, with John Ternus, incoming CEO, set to lead future earnings calls.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, projected September quarter revenue growth to decelerate to 9%-11%, impacted by a 2.5 percentage point FX headwind and supply constraints across iPhone, Mac, and iPad.
  • Tim Cook, CEO, stated that Apple reluctantly enacted price increases due to severe memory price inflation.
  • Active installed base reached an all-time record of over 2.5 billion devices, and paid subscriptions surpassed 1.5 billion.

Key metrics

  • Total Revenue $109.4 billion up 16% YoY
  • iPhone Revenue $54.3 billion up 22% YoY
  • Mac Revenue $10.4 billion up 29% YoY
  • iPad Revenue $6.2 billion down 6% YoY
  • Services Revenue $30.7 billion up 12% YoY
  • Gross Margin 50.1% up 80 bps sequentially
  • Diluted EPS $2.02 up 29% YoY
  • Active Installed Base >2.5 billion devices all-time record

Financial highlights

  • Total revenue increased 16% year-over-year to $109.4 billion, driven by Products revenue of $78.7 billion (up 18%) and Services revenue of $30.7 billion (up 12%).
  • iPhone revenue grew 22% year-over-year to $54.3 billion, and Mac revenue expanded 29% year-over-year to $10.4 billion.
  • Company gross margin reached 50.1%, up 80 basis points sequentially, which included an approximate 2 percentage point benefit from tariff refunds.
  • Diluted EPS rose 29% year-over-year to $2.02, supported by a $0.11 benefit from tariff refunds, while net income reached a June quarter record of $29.8 billion.

Guidance

  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Q4 2026 total revenue growth of 9% to 11% year-over-year, which reflects a 2.5 percentage point sequential foreign exchange headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, projects Q4 2026 iPhone revenue to grow in the mid-teens year-over-year.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, anticipates Q4 2026 Services growth to be largely similar to Q3's 12% rate after accounting for a 2.5 percentage point foreign exchange headwind.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, guides Q4 2026 gross margin to 47%-48%, including approximately 1 percentage point from a tariff refund benefit.
  • Kevan Parekh, CFO, expects Q4 2026 operating expenses between $19.1 billion and $19.4 billion.

    answered partially

    Tim Cook, CEO: Tim Cook reported steady 22% year-to-date iPhone growth with no evidence of demand pull-forward, and emphasized on-device processing as a core competitive advantage for long-term AI market opportunities.

  • CCB net income was $5.3B on revenue of $20.3B, up 8% YoY, with over 500,000 net new checking accounts.
  • AWM net income was $2B on revenue of $6.9B, up 19% YoY, with a 38% pre-tax margin and $50B of long-term net inflows.
  • Corporate net income was $4.2B on revenue of $6B.

Guidance

  • Barnum guides FY2026 NII ex-Markets to about $96.5B, up from $95B.
  • Barnum guides total NII to approximately $105.5B, with Markets NII about $9B.
  • Barnum guides adjusted expense to about $107.5B, a $2.5B increase from about $105B.
  • Barnum guides the FY2026 card net charge-off rate to approximately 3.2%, and consumer deposit growth to low single digits, unchanged.
  • Barnum guides the Q3 dividend to $1.65 per share.

Q&A

  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: What drove the NII ex-Markets raise, and why is Markets NII guided higher?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: NII ex-Markets rose from $95B to $96.5B, driven mainly by deposit balances and mix, with higher rates and better consumer betas smaller. Markets NII is up on lower financed non-interest-bearing assets in H2 and the CIB equity allocation increase, which moves about $150M of NII from ex-Markets into Markets.
    Answered directly.
  • Mike Mayo, Wells Fargo: Is operating leverage truly negative, and why is this as good as it gets?
    Jeremy Barnum, CFO: Capital markets outperformed by $6.5B in H1 with $1.5B of added expense; of the $2.5B guidance increase, $1.5B is booked and $1B is implied for H2. Dimon called the market exuberant, duration unknown.
    Answered directly, with pushback on the premise.
  • James Mitchell, Seaport Global Securities: Could expense growth slow with AI, and what is the update on regulatory proposals?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon called perpetual operating leverage a crazy notion and said growth might slow maybe in 2027 or 2028. He listed four regulatory fixes, noting market risk capital of $80B+ against a biggest quarterly loss of $1.4B.
    Answered partially, with pushback on the premise.
  • Saul Martinez, HSBC: Is AI's efficiency potential underestimated, and what drove equities?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon cited almost 1,000 AI use cases, about 50 really important, with some areas cutting jobs 30-40%, and said benefits accrue to customers so ROE won't reach 50%. Barnum, CFO, cited major IPOs, index rebalancing and Korean and Asia activity as one-off drivers.
    Answered directly, with pushback on the premise.
  • Ebrahim Poonawala, Bank of America: Why buy back stock at about 3x tangible book rather than hold excess capital, and what about M&A?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Barnum, CFO, put excess capital at about $40B and said buybacks shrink as the price rises. Dimon said the goal is deploying capital at a 17% return, called mentioning $20B last time a mistake, and said he is open to inorganic moves.
    Answered partially; no commitment on actions.
  • Matt O'Connor, Deutsche Bank: Is this as good as it gets, and is there a tipping point for deposit betas?
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon said it is close to as good as it gets. Barnum, CFO, said negative convexity in consumer deposit rates has not shown up yet but must at some point.
    Answered directly.

Unanswered

  • Erika Najarian, UBS: What characteristics is the board seeking in the next CEO, and is Dimon's tenure fixed or rolling? Dimon listed generic qualities and repeated 'several years, plus or minus', leaving timing to the board.
  • Manan Gosalia, Morgan Stanley: Is CIB capital allocation at a peak, and are there internal limits? Barnum said he would not discuss internal limit management.
  • Ebrahim Poonawala, Bank of America: What will JPM do with excess capital, including M&A? Dimon said he will not say what the firm will do.

Notable quotes

  • “The market is clearly extremely risk on, and we're kind of takers of that.” Jeremy Barnum, CFO
  • “I made a mistake last time mentioning $20 billion.” Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO
  • “I'm definitely not going to get into discussions with you about internal limit management.” Jeremy Barnum, CFO

  • Tone: Q1 emphasis on private credit, cyber risk and a $105B expense guide described as 'an outcome of business results' gave way to Q2 talk of an 'extremely risk on' market and Dimon saying conditions are close to as good as it gets.

Risks mentioned

  • Dimon saw mild weakening in competitors' credit underwriting: aggressive revenue assumptions, more PIK, weaker covenants and rollover risk.
  • Barnum flagged yield-seeking flows as a risk to consumer deposit growth and negative convexity in consumer deposit rates that has not yet appeared.

JPMorgan's fiscal Q2 2026 results showed net income of $16.9 billion and EPS of $6.14, with revenue ex-significant items up 15% YoY and expenses up 15% YoY to $27.3 billion. Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised several full-year 2026 guidance figures, including NII ex-Markets to about $96.5 billion from $95 billion and adjusted expense to about $107.5 billion from $105 billion. Management described the current environment as strong but not repeatable at peak levels.

Key takeaways

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, said IB fees were fine historically but not at peak, with some pull-forward and large deals boosting the quarter.
  • Barnum said the equities strength reflected major IPOs, index rebalancing, Korean equity dynamics and Asia activity, and that such a combination seems improbable to repeat.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, said it is 'getting close to as good as it gets' and that nobody knows how long it will last.
  • Dimon said perpetual operating leverage is not realistic and that a slowdown in expense growth may come in 2027 or 2028.
  • Barnum said consumer deposit growth is still expected to be low single-digit this year, unchanged, with yield-seeking flows a risk.

  • Expenses $27.3 billion up 15% YoY; Q1 2026: $26.9 billion

  • AWM AUM $5.1 trillion up 18% YoY

  • CCB revenue $20.3 billion, up 8% YoY, with CCB net income $5.3 billion.
  • AWM revenue $6.9 billion, up 19% YoY, with AUM of $5.1 trillion, up 18% YoY, and long-term net inflows of $50 billion.
  • Credit costs $2.5 billion, with net charge-offs of $2.4 billion and a net reserve build of $149 million; standardized CET1 ratio 14.1%, down 20 basis points QoQ.

Claude Fable 5.17.0

JPMorgan Chase reported Q2 2026 net income of $16.9 billion, EPS of $6.14 and ROTCE of 23%, with revenue ex significant items up 15% year-on-year and CIB revenue up 27% to $24.9 billion on an 86% rise in equities markets revenue. Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised full-year NII ex-Markets guidance to about $96.5 billion from $95 billion and adjusted expense to about $107.5 billion, up $2.5 billion. Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, addressed the naming of co-presidents Doug and Troy and Marianne's retirement, saying his own timetable is unchanged.

Key takeaways

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, said first-half capital markets outperformed prior expectations by $6.5 billion, with $1.5 billion of associated expense already booked and $1 billion implicitly added for the second half.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, said conditions are 'getting close to as good as it gets' and Barnum called this quarter's equities combination (major IPOs, index rebalancing, Korean market dynamics, Asia activity) statistically improbable to repeat.
  • Dimon said two co-presidents were named to prepare them for more, Marianne chose to retire, and his timetable remains several years, plus or minus, up to the board.
  • Dimon said roughly $40 billion of excess capital is expected to be deployed over time at a 17% target return, called the earlier $20 billion M&A mention a mistake, and said the firm buys back less as the share price rises.
  • Dimon said JPM has almost 1,000 AI use cases with about 50 important ones, discrete areas have reduced jobs by 30-40% with most people offered other roles, and the ultimate beneficiary will be customers.

Key metrics

  • Net income $16.9 billion including significant items; $16.5 billion in Q1
  • EPS $6.14 $5.94 in Q1
  • ROTCE 23% flat vs Q1
  • Revenue ex significant items up 15% year-on-year
  • Expenses $27.3 billion up 15% year-on-year
  • CIB revenue $24.9 billion up 27% year-on-year
  • Equities markets revenue up 86% year-on-year
  • Standardized CET1 ratio 14.1% down 20 bps quarter-on-quarter

Financial highlights

  • Expenses were $27.3 billion, up 15% year-on-year, and credit costs were $2.5 billion including $2.4 billion of net charge-offs and a $149 million reserve build.
  • CIB net income was $9.7 billion with IB fees up 30%, fixed income markets up 6% and equities markets up 86% year-on-year.
  • CCB net income was $5.3 billion on revenue of $20.3 billion, up 8%, with average deposits up 3% year-on-year and over 500,000 net new checking accounts.
  • AWM net income was $2 billion at a 38% pre-tax margin, with revenue of $6.9 billion up 19%, $50 billion of long-term net inflows and AUM of $5.1 trillion, up 18%.
  • Standardized CET1 was 14.1%, down 20 bps quarter-on-quarter, with standardized RWA up approximately $103 billion.

Guidance

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, guided full-year 2026 NII ex-Markets to about $96.5 billion (from $95 billion), total NII to approximately $105.5 billion and Markets NII to about $9 billion.
  • Barnum guided full-year adjusted expense to about $107.5 billion, raised $2.5 billion, driven mainly by volume- and revenue-related expense.
  • Barnum guided the full-year card net charge-off rate to approximately 3.2% and announced a $1.65 per share quarterly dividend effective Q3 2026.
  • Barnum kept consumer deposit growth at low single-digit for 2026, while Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, said a slowdown in expense growth might come in 2027 or 2028.
  • Dimon said smart cash tool tests will come out this year and that capital will be deployed at a 17% target return over time.

Q&A

  • John McDonald, Truist Securities: Drivers of the NII ex-Markets revision, second-half cadence and exit rate, and why Markets NII rose despite higher rates.
    Jeremy Barnum, Chief Financial Officer: NII ex-Markets moved from $95 billion to $96.5 billion, implying a higher exit run rate if forwards play out; the biggest driver is deposit balances plus mix shift toward higher margin, with consumer betas outperforming the model so actual sensitivity is probably above EAR. Markets NII is forecast up on fewer financed non-interest-bearing assets in the second half, and a higher CIB equity allocation moved roughly $150 million of NII from ex-Markets into Markets NII.

  • Mike Mayo, Wells Fargo: Challenged the negative operating leverage framing and asked why Dimon says this is as good as it gets; also asked about Troy's fit for the consumer bank.

Dimon called the market very healthy and exuberant and said Troy was evaluated on analytics, EQ and culture carrying; Barnum corrected that Troy was an options trader, not FX.
    Answered directly
  • James Mitchell, Seaport Global Securities: Whether AI could slow expense growth and improve operating leverage, and any developments on regulatory proposals.
    Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO: Dimon called perpetual operating leverage a crazy notion, said expense growth might slow in 2027 or 2028, and listed four regulatory changes sought: removing double counts in operational and market risk capital ($80 billion-plus market risk capital vs a biggest-ever quarterly loss of $1.4 billion), adjusting G-SIB as intended since 2015, and fixing short-term wholesale funding treatment. Total net interest income expectations were revised upward alongside higher revenue- and volume-driven expense projections. The firm expanded executive responsibilities across Doug and Troy as co-presidents following Marianne's planned retirement, while leadership signaled that elevated trading conditions may not persist.

Key takeaways

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, raised full-year 2026 NII ex-Markets guidance to about $96.5 billion and total NII to approximately $105.5 billion, driven by deposit balances and favorable rate dynamics.
  • Full-year adjusted expense expectations were lifted by $2.5 billion to about $107.5 billion, reflecting volume and incentive compensation against first-half capital markets outperformance.
  • CIB generated $24.9 billion in revenue, up 27% year-over-year, supported by an 86% surge in equities trading and a 30% rise in investment banking fees.

  • Jeremy Barnum, CFO, highlighted that consumer spending remains robust with delinquencies lower than expected across FICO bands, supported by a strong labor market.
  • Jamie Dimon, Chairman and CEO, stated capital rules should eliminate double counts in market and operational risk, correct short-term wholesale funding calculations, and adjust G-SIB surcharges.

  • Diluted EPS $6.14 Q2 2026 reported

  • Consumer & Community Banking revenue grew 8% year-over-year to $20.3 billion, adding over 500,000 net new checking accounts during the quarter.
  • Total credit costs were $2.5 billion, including $2.4 billion in net charge-offs and a net reserve build of $149 million.

Multiple misattributions between Dimon and Barnum, an empty unanswered section, every Q&A read marked 'answered directly', and only two of five guidance moves in what changed.

  • In the Mike Mayo exchange, the $6.5B capital markets outperformance and $1.5B of related expense are credited to Jamie Dimon; Jeremy Barnum gave those figures.
  • In the Saul Martinez exchange, the equities drivers (IPOs, index rebalancing, Korea, Asia) are credited to Jamie Dimon; Jeremy Barnum explained them.
  • "Jamie Dimon... highlighted negative convexity risk... deposit repurchases": this was Jeremy Barnum's explanation.
  • "Jeremy Barnum, CFO, cited mild credit underwriting weakening... around covenants and expense add-backs": Jamie Dimon described the mild weakening, covenants and add-backs; Barnum discussed only data center deals.
  • "Jamie Dimon... warned that exceptional equities market performance driven by short-term pull-forward and large deals is hard to repeat": Jeremy Barnum said the equities combination was hard to repeat, and he tied pull-forward to investment banking, not equities.
  • The 17% return target is credited to Jeremy Barnum in the Poonawala exchange, but Jamie Dimon said it.
  • "Berlin branch": Dimon said Chase "opened up in Berlin" as a digital bank, not a branch.
